Varadhan’s Decomposition of Shift-Invariant Closed L 2 L^{2} -forms for Large Scale Interacting Systems on Euclidean Lattices Thanks: This work was supported in part by JST CREST Grant Number JPMJCR1913, KAKENHI 18H05233, 24K21515 and the UTokyo Global Activity Support Program for Young Researchers.
Kenichi Bannai, Makiko Sasada

Abstract
. We rigorously formulate and prove for a relatively general class of interactions Varadhan’s Decomposition of shift-invariant closed L 2 L^{2} -forms for a large scale interacting system on the Euclidean lattice with finite range. Such decomposition of closed forms has played an essential role in proving the diffusive scaling limit of nongradient systems. A general expression in terms of conserved quantities was sought from observations for specific models, but a precise formulation or rigorous proof up until now had been elusive. Our result is based on a general decomposition theorem of shift-invariant closed uniform forms studied in our previous article [ BKS20 ] . In the present article, we show that the same universal structure also appears for L 2 L^{2} -forms. The essential assumptions are: (i) the set of states on each vertex is a finite set, (ii) the measure on the configuration space is the product measure, and (iii) there is a certain uniform spectral gap estimate for the mean field version of the interaction. As a special case, our result gives Varadhan’s decomposition for the case of the multi-species exclusion process, which is a new result that could not be proved by existing methods. Our result also gives complete proofs of Varadhan’s decompositions for finite range interactions, whose detailed proofs have been missing in the literature – presumably because there exists an obstruction to the standard method of proof.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 82C22, Secondary: 05C63

1.
Introduction

In the last three decades, the theory of the hydrodynamic behavior of interacting particle systems has been well developed, and a rigorous understanding of the various models has been achieved.
However, we still do not have a sufficiently conceptual understanding of the diffusive scaling limit for nongradient models. In particular, the origin of specific expressions in the
variational formula for diffusion coefficients in terms of conserved quantities was a mystery.
There are not many results for nongradient models with more than one conserved quantity, and it was not certain how common such formula holds for models with multiple conserved quantities.

In our previous article
[
BKS20
]
, we discovered that there is indeed a universal conceptual
structure for a certain general class of models which leads to a general

decomposition of shift-invariant closed uniform forms
in terms of a group action on the
configuration space and conserved quantities. If this decomposition
extends to
L^{2}
-forms, then this should also appear in the variational formula for the diffusion coefficient, and it can be said that the extension to
L^{2}
-form allows us to capture this universal structure
of nongradient models.

The purpose of this article is to formulate and prove for relatively general interactions
having a uniformly bounded spectral gap

Varadhan’s Decomposition of shift-invariant closed
L^{2}
-forms

for a large scale interacting system on an Euclidean lattice with finite range,
when the set of states on each vertex is a finite set.
As a special case, our result gives Varadhan’s decomposition for the case of the multi-species exclusion process,
which is a new result that could not be proved by existing methods. We emphasize that the number of conserved quantities of the multi-species exclusion process can be arbitrary large. For such case, the exact algebraic computation used in the existing methods to obtain the explicit expression of the decomposition is impractical.
Our result also gives a complete proof of Varadhan’s decomposition
for finite range (in particular not necessarily nearest neighbour) interactions, whose detailed proof has also been missing in the literature –
presumably because there exists an obstruction to the standard method of proof. The obstruction is discussed in detail in Remark
6.2
precisely.

In the following, we summarize our setting and main result without giving complete definitions. The precise definitions are given in later sections.

We let
S
be a finite nonempty set,
the typical example being
S=\{0,\ldots,\kappa\}
for some integer
\kappa>0
with base point
*=0
,
and we let

S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\coloneqq\prod_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}S

be the configuration space on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
We define an
interaction
to be a map
\phi\colon S\times S\rightarrow S\times S

satisfying a certain symmetry condition which expresses the interaction between states
on adjacent vertices.
Models such as the
multi-species exclusion process
and the
generalized exclusion process

may be described in a unified manner using the interaction.
Let
E\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be a set of directed edges, such that the pair
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},E)
defines a

locale
, in other words, a connected
locally finite simple symmetric directed graph.
For

\mathbb{E}^{d}\coloneqq\{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mid\sum_{j=1}^{d}|x_{j}-y_{j}|=1\},

where
x_{j}
and
y_{j}
are the
j
-th components of
x=(x_{j}),y=(y_{j})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
the graph
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\mathbb{E}^{d})
is simply the usual nearest neighbor Euclidean lattice.
The set
E
stipulates the pairs of vertices of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
where an interaction may occur.
For any configuration
\eta=(\eta_{x})\in S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
and directed edge
e=(x,y)\in E
,
we denote by
\eta^{e}
the configuration obtained by interacting
the states on the vertices of
e
. In other words,
\eta^{e}
and
\eta

coincides outside the
x
and
y
components, and if we denote by
\eta^{e}_{x}
and
\eta^{e}_{y}

the
x
and
y
components of
\eta^{e}
, then we have
(\eta^{e}_{x},\eta^{e}_{y})=\phi(\eta_{x},\eta_{y})
,
where
\eta_{x}
and
\eta_{y}
are the
x
and
y
components of
\eta
.
We call such
\eta^{e}

a transition
of
\eta
.

A
local function

f\colon S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is any function on
S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}

whose value depends on the states of the configuration at only a finite number of vertices,
and we denote by
C_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}})
the
\mathbb{R}
-linear space of local functions on
S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
.
For any local function
f
and directed edge
e\in E
, we define the differential
\nabla_{\!e}f
to be the
local function given for any
\eta\in S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
by

\nabla_{\!e}f(\eta)\coloneqq f(\eta^{e})-f(\eta).

In
[
BKS20
]
, we constructed the space of
uniform functions

C_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}})
,
a class of functions which gives a rigorous framework to consider
certain infinite sums of normalized
local functions which appear in the formulation of the decomposition of closed forms.
This space allows for the rigorous consideration of infinite sums such as

(1)

\Gamma_{f}\coloneqq\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\tau_{x}(f),

which in
[
KL99
]
*p.144, one of the most pedagogical textbooks for the hydrodynamic limit, is stated “does not make sense”.
Here,
f
is any certainly normalized local function and

\tau_{x}
denotes the translation by
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
The differential
\nabla_{\!e}
extends
\mathbb{R}
-linearly to a differential
\nabla_{\!e}\colon C_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}})\rightarrow C_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}})

giving the differential
\partial\Gamma_{f}=(\nabla_{\!e}\Gamma_{f})_{e\in E}
.

One key ingredient in the formulation of the decomposition of closed forms
are certain forms defined using the conserved quantities.
We define a
conserved quantity
to be any certainly normalized function

\xi\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\xi(s^{\prime}_{1})+\xi(s^{\prime}_{2})=\xi(s_{1})+\xi(s_{2})

for any
(s_{1},s_{2})\in S\times S
, where
(s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime})=\phi(s_{1},s_{2})
(see
Definition

2.3
).
Our key insight is that we may characterize conserved quantities simply as
invariants preserved by interaction on adjacent vertices.
If we denote by
{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)
the
\mathbb{R}
-linear space of conserved quantities,
then
{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)
is a finite dimensional space since we have assumed that
S
is finite,
and the number of linearly independent conserved quantities of the interaction
may be deduced from the dimension
c_{\phi}\coloneqq{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)
of this space.

For each
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, any conserved quantity defines a local function
\xi_{x}(\eta)\coloneqq\xi(\eta_{x})
for any

\eta=(\eta_{x})\in S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
. The infinite sum

(2)

\xi_{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\coloneqq\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\xi_{x}

defines a uniform function on
S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
which satisfies
\nabla_{\!e}\xi_{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}=0

for any
e\in E
.
We say that two configurations
\eta=(\eta_{x})
and
\eta^{\prime}=(\eta^{\prime}_{x})
have the
same conserved quantities
,
if their components
differ only at a finite set of vertices
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and for any conserved quantity
\xi
,
we have
\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\xi_{x}(\eta)=\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\xi_{x}(\eta^{\prime})
.
A
path
in a configuration space is a sequence of transitions, and we
say that an interaction is
irreducibly quantified
, if there exists a path, in the configuration
space between any two distinct configurations with the same conserved quantities.

A
uniform form
is a system
(\omega_{e})_{e\in E}
of local functions
\omega_{e}\colon S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for
e\in E

satisfying certain conditions (see
Definition

2.28
).
For a path in a configuration space, we
define the integration
\int_{\vec{\gamma}}\omega
of a form
\omega=\{\omega_{e}\}
with respect to the path
\vec{\gamma}

to be the sum of values
\omega_{e}(\eta)
over the transitions
(\eta,\eta^{e})
which appear in the path.
We say that a path is
closed
if the configuration at the beginning and the ending of the path coincide,
and we say that a form
\omega
is
closed
, if the integral of
\omega
vanishes for
any closed path in the configuration space.

Next, we assume that the set of directed edges
E
is preserved by translation

\tau_{x}
for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. This is equivalent to
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},E)
having an action of the group
G=\mathbb{Z}^{d}

by translation.
We call such
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},E)
a Euclidean lattice with
finite range
, recalling that
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},E)
is assumed to be a locally finite graph.
We say that a form is
shift-invariant
,
if it is invariant with respect to the translation.
Our notion of shift-invariant closed
L^{2}
-forms for the case of the
generalized exclusion process coincides with the notion of germs of closed
forms given in
[
KL99
]
*§Appendix 4.
The main result of our article concerns the
decomposition of shift-invariant closed
L^{2}
forms
,
which is a generalization of
[
KL99
]
*Theorem 4.14.

We first review the result of our previous article

[
BKS20
]
. We fix a basis
\xi^{(1)},\ldots,\xi^{(c_{\phi})}
of
{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)
.
The main result of
[
BKS20
]
, specialized to the case of
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},E)
is given as follows.

Theorem 1

(
[
BKS20
]
*Theorem 5)
.

Let
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},E)
be a Euclidean lattice with finite range,
which by definition has an action of the group
G=\mathbb{Z}^{d}

given by translation.
Let
\phi
be an irreducibly quantified interaction,
and assume in addition that
\phi
is simple if
d=1
.
Then for any shift-invariant closed uniform form
\omega=(\omega_{e})_{e\in E}\in\prod_{e\in E}C_{{\mathrm{loc}}}(S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}})
,
there exist unique constants
a_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}
for
i=1,\ldots,c_{\phi}
and
j=1,\ldots,d
,
and a local function
f\in C_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}})

such that for any
e\in E
, we have

\omega_{e}=\nabla_{\!e}\Bigl(\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\tau_{x}(f)+\sum_{i=1}^{c_{\phi}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{ij}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}x_{j}\xi^{(i)}_{x}\Bigr)

in
C_{{\mathrm{loc}}}(S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}})
, where
\xi^{(i)}_{x}
for any
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is the local function defined
as
\xi^{(i)}_{x}(\eta)\coloneqq\xi^{(i)}(\eta_{x})
for
\eta=(\eta_{x})\in S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
.

Here, an interaction
\phi
is
simple
if and only if
c_{\phi}=1
, and
the monoid generated by
\xi(S)
in
\mathbb{R}
for any non-zero conserved quantity
\xi
is isomorphism to
\mathbb{N}
or
\mathbb{Z}
.
In this article, we will use
1
to prove the decomposition for shift-invariant closed
L^{2}
-forms
.

Now, we take a probability measure
\nu
fully supported on
S
, i.e.
\nu(s)>0
for any
s\in S
, and
consider the product measure
\mu\coloneqq\nu^{\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
on
S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
. Also, we fix a shift-invariant
weight

r=(r_{e})_{e\in E}\in\prod_{e\in E}C_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}})
, which determines a weighted
L^{2}
-space,
such that
r_{e}(\eta)>0
for any
e\in E
and
\eta\in S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
.
When applied to prove the diffusive scaling limit of a nongradient reversible process,
\mu
is chosen to be a reversible measure for the process and the weight encodes the information of the frequency of the transition on each directed edge, hence
such data may correspond to the stochastic process given by the generator

Lf(\eta)=\sum_{e\in E}r_{e}(\eta)\nabla_{\!e}f(\eta)=\sum_{e\in E}r_{e}(\eta)(f(\eta^{e})-f(\eta)).

However, for the main theorem of this article, we do not need the reversibility nor any relation between the weight
r=(r_{e})
and the probability measure
\mu
which we consider in the main result.
Thus we take the probability measure
\mu
independently from the weight. Indeed, we do not need to consider any stochastic process in this article as the results only concern some forms in a weighted
L^{2}
-space.

We let
L^{2}(\mu)_{r_{e}}
be the weighted
L^{2}
-space
L^{2}(r_{e}d\mu)
of measurable functions on
S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}

with a norm given by
\|f\|_{r_{e}}^{2}\coloneqq E_{\mu}[r_{e}f^{2}]
. Since
r_{e}
is bounded by positive constants from above and below,
the space of local functions
C_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}})
is a dense subspace of
L^{2}(\mu)_{r_{e}}
.
We rigorously extend the notion of closed forms to
L^{2}
-forms, which is precisely given in Section
2
.
Our main result is as follows.

Theorem 2

(=
Theorem

3.9
)
.

Let the assumptions be as in Theorem
1
.
Furthermore, suppose
\nu
has a uniformly bounded
spectral gap for the interaction
(S,\phi)
in the sense of Definition
3.6
.
Furthermore, we consider a weight
r=(r_{e})_{e\in E}
invariant with respect to the action of the group
G=\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Then for any shift-invariant closed
L^{2}
-form
\omega=(\omega_{e})_{e\in E}\in\prod_{e\in E}L^{2}(\mu)_{r_{e}}
,
there exist unique constants
a_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}
for
i=1,\ldots,c_{\phi}
and
j=1,\ldots,d
,
and a sequence of local functions
f_{n}\in C_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}})
,
such that for any
e\in E
, we have

(3)

\omega_{e}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\nabla_{\!e}\Bigl(\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\tau_{x}(f_{n})+\sum_{i=1}^{c_{\phi}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{ij}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}x_{j}\xi^{(i)}_{x}\Bigr)

in
L^{2}(\mu)_{r_{e}}
.

2
is valid for the following example
of the multi-species exclusion process,
which could not be proven by existing methods.

Example 1.1
.

For an integer
\kappa>0
, let
S=\{0,1,\ldots,\kappa\}
with
*=0
, the map
given by

\phi\colon S\times S\rightarrow S\times S,\quad(s_{1},s_{2})\mapsto(s_{2},s_{1})

is an interaction which is
irreducibly quantified
(see
[
BKS20
]
*Proposition 2.27 for a proof).
This interaction gives the

multi-species exclusion process
.
We have
c_{\phi}=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)=\kappa
in this case,
and a basis of the space of conserved quantities
{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)
is given by

\xi^{(i)}\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

for
i=1,\ldots,\kappa
, such that

\xi^{(i)}(s)\coloneqq 1

if
i=s
and
\xi^{(i)}(s)\coloneqq 0
if
i\neq s
.
Let
\nu
be any probability measure fully supported on
S
.
It is well known that
\nu
has a uniformly bounded
spectral gap for the interaction
(S,\phi)
in the sense of Definition
3.6
(cf,
[
DS81
]
, Section 4.1.2. of
[
CLR10
]
, Theorem 3.4. of
[
Ca08
]
),
hence
2
holds in this case for any Euclidean lattice with finite range
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},E)
with
d>1
and any shift-invariant weight
r=(r_{e})_{e\in E}
.

This result is applicable to study the diffusive scaling limits for the following process. Fix a Euclidean lattice with finite range
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},E)
,
d>1
and a
symmetric matrix
(\lambda_{ij})\in M_{\kappa+1}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\lambda_{ij}>0
for
i,j=0,1,\ldots,\kappa
. Consider a Markov process with generator

Lf(\eta)=\sum_{e\in E}\lambda_{\eta_{o(e)},\eta_{t(e)}}(f(\eta^{e})-f(\eta)).

Then, the process is reversible for product measures
\mu=\nu^{\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
for any
\nu
and choosing
r_{e}(\eta)=\lambda_{\eta_{o(e)},\eta_{t(e)}}
,
2
provides the key decomposition for the non-gradient method for this process.

For the case where
\kappa=2
and
\lambda_{ij}=\lambda\mathbf{1}_{ij=0}
is called the two-color simple exclusion process and studied by Quastel
[
Qua92
]
. For this case, the weight is degenerate and so
2
can not be applied directly. On the other hand, since
\lambda_{10}=\lambda_{01}=\lambda_{20}=\lambda_{02}
, the color-blind process is the symmetric simple exclusion process and this property enables analysis specific to this two-color simple exclusion process.

2
is also valid for the following example
of the generalized exclusion process, which is also called the partial exclusion process.

Example 1.2
.

For an integer
\kappa>0
, let
S=\{0,1,\ldots,\kappa\}
with
*=0
, the map
given by

\phi(s_{1},s_{2})=\begin{cases}(s_{1}-1,s_{2}+1)&s_{1}>0,s_{2}<\kappa\\
(s_{1},s_{2})&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

is an interaction which is
irreducibly quantified
(see
[
BKS20
]
*Proposition 2.27 for a proof).
This interaction gives the

generalized exclusion process
.
We have
c_{\phi}=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)=1
in this case,
and a basis of the space of conserved quantities
{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)
is given by

\xi\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\xi(s)\coloneqq s.

Let
\nu
be any probability measure fully supported on
S
.
It is shown in Theorem 3.1. of
[
Ca04
]
that
\nu
has a uniformly bounded
spectral gap for the interaction
(S,\phi)
in the sense of Definition
3.6
,
hence
2
holds in this case for any Euclidean lattice with finite range
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},E)
with
d>1
and any shift-invariant weight
r=(r_{e})_{e\in E}
.

This result is applicable to study the diffusive scaling limits for the following process. Fix a Euclidean lattice with finite range
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},E)
,
d>1
and two functions
a,b:\{0,1,2,\dots,\kappa\}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
a(0)=b(0)=0
and
a(s)>0,b(s)>0
for
s\neq 0
. Consider a Markov process with generator

Lf(\eta)=\sum_{e\in E}a(\eta_{o(e)})b(\kappa-\eta_{t(e)})(f(\eta^{e})-f(\eta)).

Then, the process is reversible for a one-parameter family of product measures
\mu_{\rho}=\nu_{\rho}^{\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
determined by functions
a,b
and indexed by the density of particles
\rho
(cf. Section 2.2. of
[
SS18
]
, but note that the dynamics is symmetric in our case, so we do not need any further assumption on
a,b
). Choosing
r_{e}(\eta)=a(\eta_{o(e)})b(\kappa-\eta_{t(e)})+\mathbf{1}_{\{\eta_{o(e)}(\kappa-\eta_{t(e)})=0\}}
,
2
provides the key decomposition for the non-gradient method for this process. The indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{\{\eta_{o(e)}(\kappa-\eta_{t(e)})=0\}}
is added to make sure that
r_{e}(\eta)>0
for any
\eta
and
e\in E
, and indeed if we replace
a(\eta_{o(e)})b(\kappa-\eta_{t(e)})
by
r_{e}(\eta)
in the generator, the generator itself does not change. For the special case
a(s)=b(s)=\mathbf{1}_{\{s\geq 1\}}
on the standard Euclidean lattice
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\mathbb{E}^{d})
is studied in Section 7 of
[
KL99
]
.

To study the diffusive scaling limit, such as the hydrodynamic limit and the equilibrium fluctuation, for nongradient models, the decomposition of
2

is necessary in order to apply existing methods to general models regardless of whether we use the entropy method or the relative entropy method.
(In contrast, for the specific case of the exclusion process, a method to avoid the use of the
decomposition has recently been announced by Funaki-Gu-Wang
[
FGW24
]
).
In fact, over the past thirty years, this decomposition has been established for a variety of models. All models discussed in this paragraph satisfy
c_{\phi}=1
unless explicitly stated otherwise.
The first results in the analysis of nongradient models were obtained for the Ginzburg-Landau model in
[
Var93
]
by Varadhan, where the local state space
S=\mathbb{R}
, and for the two-color simple exclusion process
[
Qua92
]
by Quastel, where
S=\{0,1,2\}
with two conserved quantities (
c_{\phi}=2
).
Since then, the decomposition as well as the diffusive scaling limits are established for several nongradient models with a finite local state space
S
and product reversible measures, including the generalized exclusion process
[
KLO94
,
KL99
]
with a special jump rate, the exclusion process with speed change
[
FUY96
]
, the lattice gas with energy (
c_{\phi}=2
)
[
N03
]
, and a two-species exclusion process with annihilation and creation mechanism
[
Sas10
]
for which our results are all applicable. Among nongradient models with non-product reversible measures, the only rigorous result for the decomposition and the hydrodynamic limit is given
in
[
VY97
]
, where the exclusion process with certain mixing condition was studied. For models with a continuous local state space
S
, besides the Ginzburg-Landau model mentioned above, as far as we know, the decomposition theorem is only established for some energy conserving stochastic model
[
Her14
]
, anharmonic oscillators with stochastic noise
[
OS13
]
, a disordered harmonic chain with noise (where the decomposition is established for a class of “good” forms)
[
EM19
]
and an active exclusion process (
c_{\phi}=\infty
in some sense)
[
E21
]
. There is also a preprint
[
LOS15
]
in this direction. The generalization of nongradient method to nonreversible models are also known, and the most essential part of the proof, namely the decomposition of shift-invariant
closed forms, is typically reduced to the reversible case (cf.
[
Xu93
,
K98
,
FGQ04
,
Sas11
,
OS13
]
). As we do not assume the reversibility nor any relation between the measure
\mu
and the weight
r=(r_{e})
, our result is also applicable for nonreversible models.

There are many important nongradient models even with product reversible measures for which the diffusive scaling limits are not established, including the multi-species simple exclusion process (cf.
[
I18
]
)
– even in cases where the required spectral gap estimate is obtained.
In addition,
the generalization of the nongradient method from a nearest neighbor interaction version to a finite range interaction version on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
are often claimed to be “straightforward,” but in fact,
there is no clearly written proof in the literature.
Here, by the finite range interaction, we mean that the jumps of particles can be finite range, but do not mean the reversible measures have a finite range interaction. We have discovered that
when generalizing to the finite range interaction version,
there is in general an obstruction in the proof of the locality of the boundary term, as we remark in Section 6 of this article (see Remark
6.2
). Hence in this article, we avoid a direct proof and reduce
our proof of the main theorem to the case of the nearest neighbor interaction on the Euclidean lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, or in our terminology, the model on the locale
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\mathbb{E}^{d})
(see §
6.2
). One of the major advantages of creating a general formalism is the development of systematic methods to solve difficult problems by reducing to an easier case.
This allows, as in our case, proof of results even in cases where parallel method of proof fails to follow through.

With a similar spirit, we also reduce the problem to the case
r_{e}\equiv 1
in the beginning of the proof. This is possible since the set itself and its topology of
L^{2}(\mu)_{r_{e}}
is the same for any
r=(r_{e})
. This is not the case if
r=(r_{e})
is degenerate or unbounded.

The extension of the result on the Euclidean lattice
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},E)
with finite range to a general crystal lattice
(X,E)
, such as the hexagonal lattice, is a fundamental open problem, and we are currently preparing some articles for this extension. With this in mind, we write
the assertions and proofs in this article generally as possible with respect to the locale
(X,E)
. In the long term, we also hope that our main result may be extended to the case where
S
is countable infinite, continuous, and
\nabla
is a differential operator, an integral operator, and so on under suitable conditions.

As mentioned above, there have been many results on specific models, but up until now, there was no general framework for the nongradient method nor the decomposition of shift-invariant closed forms. The biggest factor that prevented full generalization was that the dimension and concrete expression of “closed but not exact forms” were obtained by calculating simultaneous linear equations in each model “on a case-by-case basis,” and there was no unified understanding of them. Such concrete calculations are impractical when the dimension of the space of conserved quantities is large, such as
c_{\phi}=100
or
10,000
. The main reason we have succeeded in a complete generalization is that we have completely solved this problem in a previous article
[
BKS20
]
, as a problem independent of probability measures. To connect the result for uniform forms to
L^{2}
-forms, in this article, we introduce several projective limits, which allow for us to make sense of infinite sums such as
\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\tau_{x}f
and
\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}x_{j}\xi^{(i)}_{x}
not only in a formal way but as a uniform function, which is a well-defined object. Also, to state the result in full generality, we define the notion of “closed forms” independent form specific models, just as the set of forms which are locally exact under restriction via conditional expectation.
With such difficulties aside,
the proofs in this article are roughly in line with the standard methods introduced by Varadhan and developed in subsequent research mentioned above. The crucial difference is the treatment of the boundary term that remains at the end. In the existing proofs, it is necessary to find the limit of the boundary term specifically for each model, but in this article, we just show that the limit is a shift-invariant uniform
closed form, and the rest can be attributed to the result of the previous article
[
BKS20
]
, thus
completely avoiding model-specific discussions. We also note that the proof of the Boundary Estimate (Proposition
4.3
), which is one of the key estimates of Varadhan’s non-gradient method, in the general setting is not
a simple extension of existing methods, since we cannot use any specific property of the model.

The rest of the article is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce our general setting and basic notions, such as uniform functions and forms as well as their norms. In this section, we consider a general locale
(X,E)
, and the existence of a group action is not assumed. In Section 3, we assume that a locale
(X,E)
has an action of a group
G
, which is free and introduce some notions, such as exact/closed shift-invariant
L^{2}
-forms. Then, we introduce the notion of the uniform spectral gap estimate, which is an essential assumption of our main theorem, and the main theorem as well. We note that the uniform spectral gap estimate condition depends only on
(S,\phi)
and
\nu
, but not on the locale
(X,E)
and the rate
r=(r_{e})
. In Section 4, we prove a few key estimates. All the results in this section hold for any locale
(X,E)
. Section 5 is devoted to the construction of converging sequence which directly gives the decomposition of
2
for the locale
(X,E)=(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\mathbb{E}^{d})
. Finally, in Section 6 we give a proof of the main theorem. We first prove the result for the locale
(X,E)=(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\mathbb{E}^{d})
, and then show that the result for more general finite range Euclidean lattice
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},E)
can be reduced to the case of the standard Euclidean lattice
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\mathbb{E}^{d})
.

2.
Co-local Functions and Forms

In this section, we introduce the data
((X,E),(S,\phi),r)
which will be used to construct our model.
We will then introduce the notion of co-local functions and forms on the configuration space of our model.

2.1.
The Large Scale Interacting System

In this subsection, we will introduce the data
((X,E),(S,\phi),r)

which we use to model the large scale interacting system.

We define a
directed graph
to be the pair
(X,E)

consisting of a set
X
which we call the
set of vertices
and
E\subset X\times X
,
which we call the
set of directed edges
. We let
o,t\colon E\rightarrow X
be the
projections to the first and second components of
X\times X
,
which we call the
origin
and the
target
of a directed edge.
We say that a graph
(X,E)
is
symmetric
,
if the opposite
\bar{e}\coloneqq(t(e),o(e))\in E
for any
e=(o(e),t(e))\in E
,
locally finite

if
E_{x}\coloneqq\{e\in E\mid o(e)=x\}
is finite for any
x\in X
,
and
simple
if
o(e)\neq t(e)
for any

e\in E
.

We define a
path
on
(X,E)
to be a finite sequence
\vec{p}\coloneqq(e^{1},\ldots,e^{N})

of edges satisfying
t(e^{i})=o(e^{i+1})
for any
0<i<N
.
We say that
\vec{p}
is a path from
o(\vec{p})\coloneqq o(e^{1})
to
t(\vec{p})\coloneqq t(e^{N})
of
length
{\mathrm{len}}(\vec{p})\coloneqq N
.
For any
x,x^{\prime}\in X
, we let
P(x,x^{\prime})
the set of paths from
x
to
x^{\prime}
.
We define the
distance
from
x
to
x^{\prime}
by

d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})\coloneqq\inf_{\vec{p}\in P(x,x^{\prime})}{\mathrm{len}}(\vec{p})

if
P(x,x^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
, and
d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})\coloneqq\infty
otherwise.
For any
Y\subset X
, we say that
Y
is
connected
, if for any
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
,
there exists a path from
y
to
y^{\prime}
consisting of edges whose origin and target are all elements of
Y
.

Definition 2.1
.

We define a
locale
to be a connected locally finite simple symmetric directed graph.
The locale represents the space underlying our large scale interacting system.

The most important example of a locale is the Euclidean lattice
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\mathbb{E}^{d})
given in §
1
.
Let
(X,E)
be a graph. For any
Y\subset X
, if we let
E_{Y}\coloneqq E\cap(Y\times Y)
,
then the pair
(Y,E_{Y})
defines a graph. A
subgraph of
(X,E)
is any graph of the form

(Y,E_{Y})

for some
Y\subset X
. In particular, if
(X,E)
is a locale and the set
Y\subset X
is connected,
then
(Y,E_{Y})
is a locale. A
sublocale of
(X,E)
is any locale of the form
(Y,E_{Y})
for some connected

Y\subset X
. A
finite locale
is a locale
(X,E)
whose vertex set
X
is finite.

As in §
1
, we define the
set of states

S
to be a finite nonempty set,
and we define the
configuration space
of
S
on
(X,E)
to be the set

S^{X}\coloneqq\prod_{x\in X}S.

An
interaction
is defined to be a map
\phi\colon{S\times S}\rightarrow{S\times S}
,
satisfying
\bar{\phi}\circ\phi(s_{1},s_{2})=(s_{1},s_{2})
for any
(s_{1},s_{2})\in S\times S

such that
\phi(s_{1},s_{2})\neq(s_{1},s_{2})
, where we let

\bar{\phi}\coloneqq\iota\circ\phi\circ\iota

for the bijection
\iota\colon S\times S\rightarrow S\times S
obtained by
exchanging the components of
S\times S
. The pair
(S,\phi)
describes
the set of all possible states on a single vertex and the possible interactions
between adjacent vertices.

Example 2.2
.

Let
S=\{0,1,2,\ldots,\kappa\}
for some integer
\kappa>0
.

(a)

The map
\phi=\iota\colon S\times S\rightarrow S\times S

above
obtained by exchanging the components
of
S\times S
defines an interaction giving the
multi-species exclusion process
.

(b)

The map given by

\phi(s_{1},s_{2})=\begin{cases}(s_{1}-1,s_{2}+1)&s_{1}>0,s_{2}<\kappa\\
(s_{1},s_{2})&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

is also an interaction, giving the
generalized exclusion process
.

See
[
BKS20
]
*Example 2.18 for other examples of interactions.

We next introduce a certain invariant called the
conserved quantity

associated to the pair
(S,\phi)
.

Definition 2.3
.

We fix an element
*\in S
which we call the
base state
.
We define a
conserved quantity
, to be any map
\xi\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

satisfying
\xi(*)=0
and

\xi(s^{\prime}_{1})+\xi(s^{\prime}_{2})=\xi(s_{1})+\xi(s_{2})

for any
(s_{1},s_{2})\in S\times S
, where
(s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}_{2})\coloneqq\phi(s_{1},s_{2})
.

We denote by
{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}_{*}(S)
the set of all conserved quantities, which has a natural structure
of an
\mathbb{R}
-linear space. Given another base state
*^{\prime}\in S
, we have a canonical
\mathbb{R}
-linear
isomorphism
{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}_{*}(S)\cong{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}_{*^{\prime}}(S)
mapping any
\xi\in{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}_{*}(S)
to
\xi^{\prime}\coloneqq\xi-\xi(*^{\prime})
.
Hence
{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}_{*}(S)
is independent up to canonical isomorphism of the choice of the base state.
For this reason, we will simply denote
{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}_{*}(S)
by
{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)
. The dimension

c_{\phi}\coloneqq\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)

is independent of the choice of the base state, and describes the number of independent
conserved quantities of the system.

The data
((X,E),(S,\phi))
above gives the geometric data underlying our interacting system.
We next give some construction associated to this data.
For any locale
(X,E)
and the pair
(S,\phi)
, we call any
\eta\in S^{X}=\prod_{x\in X}S

a
configuration
.
For any element
\eta=(\eta_{x})\in S^{X}
and
e\in E
, we denote by
\eta^{e}

the element
\eta^{e}=(\eta^{e}_{x})\in S^{X}
such that

\eta^{e}_{x}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\eta_{x}&x\neq o(e),t(e)\\
\eta^{\prime}_{x}&x=o(e),t(e),\\
\end{cases}

where
(\eta^{\prime}_{o(e)},\eta^{\prime}_{t(e)})=\phi(\eta_{o(e)},\eta_{t(e)})
.
We define a subset
\Phi_{E}
of
S^{X}\times S^{X}
by

\Phi_{E}\coloneqq\{(\eta,\eta^{e})\mid\eta\in S^{X},e\in E\}\subset S^{X}\times S^{X}.

Then the pair
(S^{X},\Phi_{E})
is a graph, which we call the
configuration space with transition structure
.

Lemma 2.4
.

The graph
(S^{X},\Phi_{E})
is a symmetric directed graph.

Proof.

This is proved in
[
BKS20
]
*Lemma 2.5, and can be seen from the fact that
for any
\eta\in S^{X}
and
e\in E
, if
\eta^{e}\neq\eta
, then from the condition
on
\phi
, we have
(\eta^{e})^{\bar{e}}=\eta
,
hence
(\eta^{e},\eta)=(\eta^{e},(\eta^{e})^{\bar{e}})\in\Phi_{E}
.
∎

The configuration space
S^{X}
describes all of the possible configuration of states
of our system, and the transition structure describes all of the possible transitions which
may occur at an instance in time. A condition that we often consider for the pair

(S,\phi)
is the following.

Definition 2.5
.

We say that an interaction
(S,\phi)
is
irreducibly quantified
, if it
satisfies the following property: For any finite locale
(X,E)
and any
configurations
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in S^{X}
, if
\sum_{x\in X}\xi(\eta_{x})=\sum_{x\in X}\xi(\eta^{\prime}_{x})

for any conserved quantity
\xi\in{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)
, then there exists a path
\vec{\gamma}
from

\eta
to
\eta^{\prime}
in
S^{X}
.

Irreducibly quantified
is equivalent to the condition that the associated stochastic process on any finite locale
(X,E)
with fixed conserved quantities is
irreducible
. This condition plays an important role in the proof of the main theorem.

Example 2.6
.

The interactions in Example
2.2
are both irreducibly quantified.
We let
*=0
be the base state of
S=\{0,\ldots,\kappa\}
.

(a)

For the multi-species exclusion process, we have
c_{\phi}=\kappa
, and a basis of
{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)

is given by
\xi^{(1)},\ldots,\xi^{(\kappa)}\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\xi^{(i)}(s)=\delta_{si}
for
i=1,\ldots,\kappa
,
where
\delta_{si}\coloneqq 1
if
s=i
and
\delta_{si}\coloneqq 0
if
s\neq i
.

(b)

For the generalized exclusion process, we have
c_{\phi}=1
, and a basis of
{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)

is given by
\xi\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
\xi(s)=s
.

See
[
BKS20
]
*Example 2.18 for other examples of irreducibly quantified interactions.

Next, we introduce certain metric like data called the
weight
.
For any
\Lambda\subset X
let
C(S^{\Lambda})\coloneqq{\mathrm{Map}}(S^{\Lambda},\mathbb{R})

be the
\mathbb{R}
-linear space of
\mathbb{R}
-valued maps on
S^{\Lambda}
.
The natural projection
S^{X}\rightarrow S^{\Lambda}
induces an injection
C(S^{\Lambda})\hookrightarrow C(S^{X})
,
which allows us to view any
f\in C(S^{\Lambda})
as a function on
S^{X}
whose value on
\eta=(\eta_{x})

depends only on the components for
x\in\Lambda
. A
local function
on
S^{X}

is any function in
C(S^{\Lambda})
for a finite
\Lambda\subset X
.

Definition 2.7
.

We define the
weight
of the system
((X,E),(S,\phi))
to be a family of local
functions
r=(r_{e})_{e\in E}
satisfying
r_{e}(\eta)>0
for any
e\in E
and
\eta\in S^{X}
.

In terms of application to the diffusive scaling limit for nongradient models, the weight should be computed by the frequency of each transition from
\eta
to
\eta^{e}
, which is so-called the jump rate, and it is natural to consider an invariant or stationary probability measure
\mu
on
S^{X}
for the process given by this jump rate. However, since we can prove the main result
without the stationarity nor reversibility condition, and instead require
\mu
to be a product measure, we choose a probability measure

\nu
on
S
, satisfying
\nu(\{s\})>0
for any
s\in S
independently from the weight
r
, and consider the
product measure
\mu\coloneqq\nu^{\otimes X}
.

Remark 2.8
.

For any given interaction
(S,\phi)
, locale
(X,E)
and probability measure
\nu
fully supported on
S
, we can construct a large class of processes which are reversible with respect to
\mu=\nu^{\otimes X}
.

The following constants will be used in the calculation of the bound of norms later.

Definition 2.9
.

For an interaction
(S,\phi)
and a probability measure
\nu
fully supported on
S
, we define

(4)

C_{\phi,\nu}:=\max_{(s_{1},s_{2})\in S^{2}}\max\left\{\frac{\nu(s^{\prime}_{1})\nu(s^{\prime}_{2})}{\nu(s_{1})\nu(s_{2})},\frac{\nu(s_{1})\nu(s_{2})}{\nu(s^{\prime}_{1})\nu(s^{\prime}_{2})}\right\}

where
(s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}_{2})=\phi(s_{1},s_{2})
. Since
S
is finite and
\nu
is supported on
S
,
we have
1\leq C_{\phi,\nu}<\infty
.

2.2.
Uniform Functions

We will study the property of the configuration space with transition structure using
a class of functions which we call the
uniform co-local functions
.
This is a variant of the uniform function originally defined in
[
BKS20
]
*§3.
In this subsection, we will review the definition of co-local functions and forms,
as well as the definition of uniform functions. Detailed proofs of the results
of this section are given in
[
BS21
]
*§2 and §3.

Let
(X,E)
be a locale, and let
\mathscr{I}
be the set of finite subsets of
X
.
For any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
, as in §
2.1
, we let
C(S^{\Lambda})={\mathrm{Map}}(S^{\Lambda},\mathbb{R})
be the

\mathbb{R}
-linear space of
\mathbb{R}
-valued functions on
S^{\Lambda}
. We may regard
any
f\in C(S^{\Lambda})
as a function on
S^{X}
whose value depends only
on the components of
\eta=(\eta_{x})
for
x\in\Lambda
.
For any
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathscr{I}
such that
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
, the natural projection

S^{\Lambda^{\prime}}\rightarrow S^{\Lambda}
induces an inclusion
C(S^{\Lambda})\hookrightarrow C(S^{\Lambda^{\prime}})
,
which forms a direct system with respect to inclusions in
\mathscr{I}
.

Definition 2.10
.

We define the
space of local functions
by

C_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{X})\coloneqq\varinjlim_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}C(S^{\Lambda})=\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}C(S^{\Lambda}),

where the direct limit is taken with respect to the inclusions
C(S^{\Lambda})\hookrightarrow C(S^{\Lambda^{\prime}})
.

We let
\mathcal{F}
be the set of subsets of
S
, which is a
\sigma
-algebra.
For any
\Lambda\subset X
, we let
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}^{\otimes\Lambda}
be the product

\sigma
-algebra on
S^{\Lambda}
obtained from
\mathcal{F}
. We note that
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}
is the sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathcal{F}_{X}
generated by the projection
S^{X}\rightarrow S^{\Lambda}
.

Next, let
\mu

be a probability measure on
(S^{X},\mathcal{F}_{X})
.
For any
\Lambda\subset X
, we define
C(S^{\Lambda}_{\mu})
to be the
\mathbb{R}
-linear space of
\mathbb{R}
-valued

measurable
functions on
(S^{\Lambda},\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda})
. If
\Lambda
is finite, then we have
C(S^{\Lambda}_{\mu})=C(S^{\Lambda})
,
since we have assumed that
S
is finite.
The natural projection
S^{X}\rightarrow S^{\Lambda}
induces an inclusion
C(S^{\Lambda})\hookrightarrow C(S^{X}_{\mu})
.

For any
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathscr{I}
such that
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
and any

integrable
function
f\in C(S^{\Lambda^{\prime}})
, we let

\pi^{\Lambda}f\coloneqq E_{\mu}[f|\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}]

be the
conditional expectation
associated to
the projection
{\mathrm{pr}}_{\Lambda}\colon S^{\Lambda^{\prime}}\rightarrow S^{\Lambda}
. Note that for any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
and
f\in C(S^{\Lambda})
,
f
is integrable since
S
is finite.
If
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathscr{I}
, then
\pi^{\Lambda}
induces the homomorphism

\pi^{\Lambda}\colon C(S^{\Lambda^{\prime}})\rightarrow C(S^{\Lambda}).

Note that for any
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda^{\prime\prime}
and integrable function
f\in C(S^{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}})
,
the tower property of the conditional expectation gives

\pi^{\Lambda}f=\pi^{\Lambda}(\pi^{\Lambda^{\prime}}f)
, which implies that
\pi^{\Lambda}
satisfies the compatibility
condition for the projective system.
Hence as in
[
BS21
]
*Definition 1.4, we may
define the space of co-local functions
C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})

as follows.

Definition 2.11
.

We define the space of
co-local functions
by

C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\coloneqq\varprojlim_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}C(S^{\Lambda}),

where the projective limit is taken with respect to the projections
\pi^{\Lambda}
.
We call any element in
C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
a co-local function.

By definition, a co-local function
f=(f^{\Lambda})\in C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
is a system of functions
f^{\Lambda}\in C(S^{\Lambda})

for
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
such that
\pi^{\Lambda}f^{\Lambda^{\prime}}=f^{\Lambda}
for any
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
. In other words,
co-local functions are
Martingales
indexed by
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
for the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda})_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}
.
Any integrable function
f\in C(S^{X}_{\mu})
defines the co-local function
(\pi^{\Lambda}f)\in C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.
This gives an injection

C_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{X})\hookrightarrow C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu}).

When
\mu
is a product measure
\mu=\nu^{\otimes X}
where
\nu
is a probability measure fully supported on
S
, the co-local functions may be expanded uniquely as an infinite sum of certain local functions.

Proposition 2.12

(
[
BS21
]
*Proposition 2.3)
.

Suppose
\mu
is a product measure
\mu=\nu^{\otimes X}
on
S^{X}
,
where
\nu
is a probability measure
fully supported on
S
.
For any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
, let

C_{\Lambda}(S^{X}_{\mu})\coloneqq\{f\in C(S^{\Lambda})\mid\pi^{\Lambda^{\prime}}f\equiv 0\text{ if }\Lambda\not\subset\Lambda^{\prime}\ \forall\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathscr{I}\}.

Then for any co-local function

(f^{\Lambda})\in C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
, there exists a unique family of functions
f_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}\in C_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}(S^{X}_{\mu})

such that

(5)

f^{\Lambda}=\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\subset\Lambda}f_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}

for any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
.

Proof.

See the proof of
[
BS21
]
*Proposition 2.3 for details.
We let
f_{\emptyset}\coloneqq f^{\emptyset}=E_{\mu}[f]
for the case
\Lambda=\emptyset
,
and we define inductively with respect to the number of elements of
\Lambda
as

f_{\Lambda}\coloneqq f^{\Lambda}-\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\subsetneq\Lambda}f_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}

for any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
. To prove that
f_{\Lambda}\in C_{\Lambda}(S^{X}_{\mu})
and satisfies the desired property,
we use the fact proved in
[
BS21
]
*Lemma 2.2
that for
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
and
\Lambda^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\subset\Lambda
, we have

\pi^{\Lambda^{\prime}}(\pi^{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}f)=\pi^{\Lambda^{\prime}\cap\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}f

for any
f\in C(S^{\Lambda})
, which is true since
\mu
is a product measure.
∎

Remark 2.13
.

Though we do not use the fact in this article, Proposition
2.12
holds for
\mu=\Pi_{x\in X}\nu_{x}
where
\nu_{x}
is a fully supported probability measure on
S
for any
x\in X
. Actually, the proof above works directly. This will be useful in future works for the cases with inhomogeneous invariant measures.

In the rest of the article, we always assume that
\mu
is a product measure
\mu=\nu^{\otimes X}
for some probability measure
\nu
fully supported on
S
.

For any co-local function
f=(f^{\Lambda})
, we will often write

f=\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{I}}f_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}

to imply that (
5
) holds for any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
.
We let
C^{0}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\coloneqq\{f\in C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\mid f_{\emptyset}=f^{\emptyset}=0\}
,
which we call the space of co-local functions of
mean zero
.

We define the
diameter
of
\Lambda\subset X
by

\operatorname{diam}(\Lambda)\coloneqq\sup_{x,x^{\prime}\in\Lambda}d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})
.
As in
[
BS21
]
*Definition 2.4, we define the space of uniform co-local functions as follows.

Definition 2.14
.

We say that a co-local function
f
is
uniform co-local
,
if there exists
R\geq 0
such that the expansion of Proposition
2.12

is given by

(6)

f=\sum_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}_{R}}f_{\Lambda},

where we denote by
\mathscr{I}_{R}
the set consisting of
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
such that
\operatorname{diam}(\Lambda)\leq R
.
We call the infimum of such
R
the
diameter
of
f
.
We denote by
C_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
the
\mathbb{R}
-linear space of uniform co-local functions,
and by
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
the subspace of
normalized

uniform co-local functions satisfying
f_{\emptyset}=f^{\emptyset}=0
.

We will next give an algebraic construction of the space of uniform co-local functions.
Namely, we will review the construction of the space of uniform functions

C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*})
denoted

C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})
in
[
BKS20
]
*Definition 3.5. We will show in Proposition

2.18
that there exists a canonical isomorphism which we call the

renormalization
between
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*})
and
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.

We fix a point
*\in S
which we call the
base point
, and we denote by
\star\in S^{X}

the configuration whose components are all at base state.
For any configuration
\eta\in S^{X}
, we define the support of
\eta
by
{\mathrm{Supp}}(\eta)\coloneqq\{x\in X\mid\eta_{x}\neq*\}
,
and we let

S^{X}_{*}\coloneqq\{\eta\in S^{X}\mid|{\mathrm{Supp}}(\eta)|<\infty\}.

We let

C(S^{X}_{*})\coloneqq{\mathrm{Map}}(S^{X}_{*},\mathbb{R})
to be the
\mathbb{R}
-linear space of maps from
S^{X}_{*}
to
\mathbb{R}
.

For any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
, the projection
S^{X}_{*}\rightarrow S^{\Lambda}
induces an inclusion
C(S^{\Lambda})\hookrightarrow C(S^{X}_{*})
, hence we may view
C_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{X})
as an
\mathbb{R}
-linear subspace of
C(S^{X}_{*})
.
For any
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathscr{I}
such that
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
, we have an inclusion

\iota_{\Lambda}\colon S^{\Lambda}\hookrightarrow S^{\Lambda^{\prime}}
given by extending any configuration

\eta\in S^{\Lambda}
by
\eta_{x}\coloneqq*
for
x\in\Lambda^{\prime}\setminus\Lambda
.
This induces a projection
\iota^{\Lambda}\colon C(S^{\Lambda^{\prime}})\rightarrow C(S^{\Lambda})
. In particular, for any
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathscr{I}
, the projection
\iota^{\Lambda^{\prime}}:C(S^{\Lambda})\rightarrow C(S^{\Lambda^{\prime}})
is well-defined as
\iota^{\Lambda^{\prime}}=\iota^{\Lambda^{\prime}}\circ\iota_{\Lambda^{\prime}\cup\Lambda}
.

Definition 2.15
.

For any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
, we define the space of local functions with
exact support
\Lambda
by

C_{\Lambda}(S^{X}_{*})\coloneqq\{f\in C(S^{\Lambda})\mid\iota^{\Lambda^{\prime}}f\equiv 0\text{ if }\Lambda\not\subset\Lambda^{\prime}\ \forall\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathscr{I}\}.

We have an expansion of functions in
C(S^{X}_{*})
via local functions with exact support as follows.

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
(f_{\Lambda})_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}
be a family of functions
f_{\Lambda}\in C_{\Lambda}(S^{X}_{*})
. Then

f\coloneqq\sum_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}f_{\Lambda}

defines a function in
C(S^{X}_{*})
. Conversely,
for any function
f\in C(S^{X}_{*})
, there exists a unique family of functions
f^{*}_{\Lambda}\in C_{\Lambda}(S^{X}_{*})

such that

(7)

f=\sum_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}f^{*}_{\Lambda}.

Proof.

The first statement is
[
BKS20
]
*Lemma 3.2, and follows from the fact that
f(\eta)

for any
\eta\in S^{X}_{*}
is a finite sum since
f_{\Lambda}(\eta)\neq 0

only if
\Lambda\subset{\mathrm{Supp}}(\eta)
.
The second statement is
[
BKS20
]
*Proposition 3.3, noting that

C_{\Lambda}(S^{X}_{*})=\{f\in C(S^{\Lambda})\mid f(\eta)=0\text{ if $\exists x\in\Lambda$ such that $\eta_{x}=*$}\}.

For any
f\in C(S^{X}_{*})
, we let

f^{*}_{\emptyset}\coloneqq\iota^{\emptyset}f=f(\star)
for the case
\Lambda=\emptyset
.
We then define
f^{*}_{\Lambda}
by induction on the order of
\Lambda
as

f^{*}_{\Lambda}\coloneqq\iota^{\Lambda}f-\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\subsetneq\Lambda}f^{*}_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}

for any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
. This construction gives the desired property.
∎

By
[
BS21
]
*Lemma 1.3, any function
C(S^{X}_{*})
may be expressed as a projective limit
of local functions. Hence we will refer to a function in
C(S^{X}_{*})
as a
pro-local function
.
As in
[
BKS20
]
*Definition 3.5, we define the space of uniform functions as follows.

Definition 2.17
.

We say that a function
f\in C(S^{X}_{*})
is
uniform pro-local
,
if there exists
R\geq 0
such that the expansion of Proposition
2.16

is given by

f=\sum_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}_{R}}f^{*}_{\Lambda}.

We call the infimum of such
R
the
diameter
of
f
.
We denote by
C_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*})
the set of all uniform pro-local functions in
C(S^{X}_{*})
,
and by
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*})
the subspace of
normalized

uniform pro-local functions satisfying
f^{*}_{\emptyset}=\iota^{\emptyset}f=f(\star)=0
.

Let
\xi\in{\mathrm{Consv}}^{\phi}(S)
be a conserved quantity. Then for any
x\in X
,
the function
\xi_{x}\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
given by
\xi_{x}(\eta)\coloneqq\xi(\eta_{x})
for any
\eta\in S^{X}

defines a function in
C_{\{x\}}(S^{X}_{*})
. By Proposition
2.16
, the infinite sum

\xi_{X}\coloneqq\sum_{x\in X}\xi_{x}

defines a uniform pro-local function of diameter
0
on
S^{X}
.

We define the
renormalization
as follows.

Proposition 2.18
.

We have a canonical isomorphism which we call the renormalization

(8)

\mathscr{R}\colon C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*})\cong C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{\mu}).

Proof.

Let
f\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*})\subset C^{0}(S^{X}_{*})
. Then by Proposition
2.16

and the definition of uniform pro-local functions given in Definition
2.17
, there exists
R\geq 0
such that

f=\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{I}_{R}}f^{*}_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}.

By Proposition
2.12
, for each
\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{I}_{R}
, there is the unique expansion
of
f^{*}_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}
considered as a co-local function as

f^{*}_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}=\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}f^{*}_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}},

where
f^{*}_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}}\in C_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.
Now, for each
\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathscr{I}
such that
\Lambda^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, we construct
\mathscr{R}(f)_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
by

\mathscr{R}(f)_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{I}_{R}\\
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}f^{*}_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}}.

Note that since the number of
\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{I}_{R}
such that
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda^{\prime\prime}
is finite,

\mathscr{R}(f)_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
gives a function in
C_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.
We define
\mathscr{R}(f)\in C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
by

\mathscr{R}(f)=\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathscr{I}^{0}}\mathscr{R}(f)_{\Lambda^{\prime}},

where
\mathscr{I}^{0}\coloneqq\mathscr{I}\setminus\{\emptyset\}
.
Note that
\mathscr{R}(f)_{\Lambda^{\prime}}=0
if
\operatorname{diam}(\Lambda^{\prime})>R
, hence
\mathscr{R}(f)
is a co-local function in
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{\mu})

as desired. We may construct the inverse isomorphism of
\mathscr{R}
in a similar manner, proving that (
8
)
is an isomorphism.
∎

Given two base states
*,*^{\prime}\in S
, one may also construct a canonical
isomorphism
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*})\cong C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*^{\prime}})
by the same manner as the last proposition.
In what follows, we will denote
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*})
simply as
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})
, and will regard
any element
f\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})
also as elements in
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*^{\prime}})
or
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{\mu})

through the renormalization isomorphisms.

Definition 2.19
.

A
uniform function
is any element of
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})
or
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
, where they are identified by the canonical
isomorphism.

2.3.
Uniform Forms

In this subsection, we consider the space of forms on the configuration space
with transition structure.
For any connected
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
, the
graph
(\Lambda,E_{\Lambda})
is a finite locale. We let
(S^{\Lambda},\Phi_{E_{\Lambda}})
be the associated
configuration space with transition structure, where

\Phi_{E_{\Lambda}}\coloneqq\{(\eta,\eta^{e})\mid\eta\in S^{\Lambda},e\in E_{\Lambda}\}.

For any
\varphi=(\eta,\eta^{e})\in\Phi_{E_{\Lambda}}
, let
\overline{\varphi}\coloneqq(\eta^{e},\eta)
, which by Lemma
2.4

is an element in
\Phi_{E_{\Lambda}}
.

Definition 2.20
.

We define
the space of forms
on
S^{\Lambda}
by

C^{1}(S^{\Lambda})\coloneqq{\mathrm{Map}}^{\mathrm{alt}}(\Phi_{E_{\Lambda}},\mathbb{R}),

where
{\mathrm{Map}}^{\mathrm{alt}}(\Phi_{E_{\Lambda}},\mathbb{R})\coloneqq\{\omega\in{\mathrm{Map}}(\Phi_{E_{\Lambda}},\mathbb{R})\mid\omega(\overline{\varphi})=-\omega(\varphi)\}
.

We have a natural inclusion

(9)

C^{1}(S^{\Lambda})\hookrightarrow\prod_{e\in E_{\Lambda}}C(S^{\Lambda})

given by
\omega\mapsto(\omega_{e})_{e\in E_{\Lambda}}
, where
\omega_{e}\in C(S^{\Lambda})

is the map given by
\omega_{e}(\eta)\coloneqq\omega((\eta,\eta^{e}))

for any
e\in E_{\Lambda}
and
\eta\in S^{\Lambda}
.
The image of any
\omega\in C^{1}(S^{\Lambda})
with respect to the
inclusion (
9
)
consists of
(\omega_{e})
satisfying
\omega_{e}(\eta)=0
if
\eta^{e}=\eta
,

\omega_{e}(\eta)=-\omega_{\bar{e}}(\eta^{e})
if
\eta^{e}\neq\eta
, and
\omega_{e}(\eta)=\omega_{e^{\prime}}(\eta)

if
\eta^{e}=\eta^{e^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 2.21
.

For any
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathscr{I}
such that
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
, the projection

\pi^{\Lambda}\colon C(S^{\Lambda^{\prime}})\rightarrow C(S^{\Lambda})
induces via (
9
)
an homomorphism

\pi^{\Lambda}\colon C^{1}(S^{\Lambda^{\prime}})\rightarrow C^{1}(S^{\Lambda})

given by
(\pi^{\Lambda}\omega)_{e}=\pi^{\Lambda}\omega_{e}
for
\omega\in C^{1}(S^{\Lambda^{\prime}})
and
e\in E_{\Lambda}
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
BS21
]
*Lemma 3.4,
[
BS21
]
*Lemma 3.5,
and the fact that we have assumed that
\mu
is the product measure.
∎

Using Lemma
2.21
, we define the space of co-local forms as follows.

Definition 2.22
.

We define
the space of co-local forms
on
S^{\Lambda}
by

C^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\coloneqq\varprojlim_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}C^{1}(S^{\Lambda}),

where the projective limit is taken with respect to the projections
\pi^{\Lambda}
.

The inclusion (
9
) gives the inclusion

(10)

C^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\hookrightarrow\prod_{e\in E}C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu}).

For any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
, we define the differential

(11)

\partial_{\Lambda}\colon C(S^{\Lambda})\rightarrow C^{1}(S^{\Lambda})

to be the
\mathbb{R}
-linear homomorphism given by
\partial_{\Lambda}f(\varphi)\coloneqq f(t(\varphi))-f(o(\varphi))
.
The image of
\partial_{\Lambda}f(\varphi)
with respect to the inclusion (
9
)
coincides with
(\nabla_{\!e}f)_{e\in E_{\Lambda}}
, where
\nabla_{\!e}f(\eta)\coloneqq f(\eta^{e})-f(\eta)

for any
\eta\in S^{\Lambda}
. Since the measure
\mu
is a product measure, we have the following.

Proposition 2.23
.

The projection
\pi^{\Lambda}
is compatible with the differentials
\partial_{\Lambda}
on
C(S^{\Lambda})
, namely
\partial_{\Lambda}\pi^{\Lambda}f=\pi^{\Lambda}\partial_{\Lambda^{\prime}}f
for any
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
and
f\in C(S^{\Lambda^{\prime}})
.
Hence the differential on each
C(S^{\Lambda})
induces the differential

\partial\colon C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\rightarrow C^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})

such that
(\partial f)^{\Lambda}=\partial_{\Lambda}f^{\Lambda}
for
f=(f^{\Lambda})\in C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
BS21
]
*Proposition 3.7, noting that a product measure is

ordinary
, as shown in
[
BS21
]
*Lemma 3.4.
∎

Since the projection
\pi^{\Lambda}
on forms is induced from the embedding (
9
),
the projection is also compatible with
\nabla_{\!e}
for any
e\in E_{\Lambda}
. This implies that
\nabla_{\!e}
induces
a differential

(12)

\nabla_{\!e}\colon C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\rightarrow C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})

such that we have
\partial f=(\nabla_{\!e}f)_{e\in E}\in C^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\subset\prod_{e\in E}C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})

for any
f\in C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.

We next define the notion of closed forms.
Let
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
, and let
(S^{\Lambda},\Phi_{E_{\Lambda}})
be a configuration space with transition structure
for a pair
(S,\phi)
. Recall that a path in
(S^{\Lambda},\Phi_{E_{\Lambda}})
is a sequence of transitions

\vec{\gamma}=(\varphi^{1},\ldots,\varphi^{N})
in
\Phi_{E_{\Lambda}}
such that
t(\varphi^{i})=o(\varphi^{i+1})

for any integer
0<i<N
. For any form
\omega\in C^{1}(S^{\Lambda})
, we define the integration
of
\omega
with respect to the path
\vec{\gamma}
by

\int_{\vec{\gamma}}\omega\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\omega(\varphi^{i}).

We say that a path
\vec{\gamma}
is
closed
, if

t(\vec{\gamma})=o(\vec{\gamma})
, where
o(\vec{\gamma})=o(\varphi^{1})
and
t(\vec{\gamma})=t(\varphi^{N})
.
As in
[
BKS20
]
*Definition 2.14, we define the closed forms on
(S^{\Lambda},\Phi_{E_{\Lambda}})
as follows.

Definition 2.24
.

We say that a form
\omega\in C^{1}(S^{\Lambda})
is
closed
, if

\int_{\vec{\gamma}}\omega=0

for any closed path
\vec{\gamma}
in
(S^{\Lambda},\Phi_{E_{\Lambda}})
.

We denote by
Z^{1}(S^{\Lambda})
the
\mathbb{R}
-linear space of closed forms in
C(S^{\Lambda})
.
The notion of closed forms is compatible with the projections
\pi^{\Lambda}
.

Lemma 2.25
.

The projections
\pi^{\Lambda}
induce
\mathbb{R}
-linear homomorphisms

\pi^{\Lambda}\colon Z^{1}(S^{\Lambda^{\prime}})\rightarrow Z^{1}(S^{\Lambda})

for any
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{I}
such that
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This is
[
BS21
]
*Lemma 3.10, since a product measure is
ordinary

by
[
BS21
]
*Lemma 3.4.
∎

Definition 2.26
.

We define the space of
closed co-local forms

Z^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
by

Z^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\coloneqq\varprojlim_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}Z^{1}(S^{\Lambda}),

where the limit is the projective limit with respect to the projections
\pi^{\Lambda}
.

By
[
BKS20
]
*Lemma 2.14, we see that for any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
and
f\in C(S^{\Lambda})
, we have

\partial_{\Lambda}f\in Z^{1}(S^{\Lambda})
, where
\partial_{\Lambda}
is the differential defined in
(
11
). Moreover, by
[
BKS20
]
*Lemma 2.15,
the differential
\partial_{\Lambda}\colon C(S^{\Lambda})\rightarrow Z^{1}(S^{\Lambda})
is surjective
and induces the isomorphism

(13)

C(S^{\Lambda})/{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\partial_{\Lambda}\cong Z^{1}(S^{\Lambda}).

The compatibility of the differential
\partial_{\Lambda}
with the projection
\pi^{\Lambda}
gives the following.

Proposition 2.27
.

The differential
\partial\colon C^{0}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\rightarrow C^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})

of Proposition
2.23

induces the isomorphism

C^{0}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})/H^{0}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\cong Z^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu}),

where
H^{0}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\coloneqq\varprojlim_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\partial_{\Lambda}
.

Proof.

This is
[
BS21
]
*Proposition 3.13,
noting that a product measure is
ordinary
by
[
BS21
]
*Lemma 3.4.
The proof follows from the fact that the projective system
\{{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\partial_{\Lambda}\}
satisfies
the Mittag-Leffler condition, which follows immediately from the fact that

{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\partial_{\Lambda}
is finite dimensional for any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
, since
we have assumed that
S
is finite.
∎

Next, we consider the differential of uniform functions.
Again, fix a base state
*\in S
.
We let
C^{1}(S^{X}_{*})\coloneqq{\mathrm{Map}}^{\mathrm{alt}}(\Phi^{*}_{E},\mathbb{R})
, where
\Phi^{*}_{E}\coloneqq\Phi_{E}\cap(S^{X}_{*}\cap S^{X}_{*})
.
Similarly to the case of (
9
), there exists an embedding

(14)

C^{1}(S^{X}_{*})\hookrightarrow\prod_{e\in E}C(S^{X}_{*})

mapping
\omega\in C^{1}(S^{X}_{*})
to
(\omega_{e})_{e\in E}
, where
\omega_{e}(\eta)\coloneqq\omega((\eta,\eta^{e}))

for any
e\in E
and
\eta\in C(S^{X}_{*})
.
We define the differential

\partial\colon C^{0}(S^{X}_{*})\rightarrow C^{1}(S^{X}_{*})

by
\partial f(\varphi)\coloneqq f(t(\varphi))-f(o(\varphi))
. Again, we have
(\partial f)_{e}=\nabla_{\!e}f

through the inclusion (
14
),
where
\nabla_{\!e}f(\eta)=f(\eta^{e})-f(\eta)
for any
\eta\in S^{X}_{*}
.

We next define the notion of uniform forms.
For any
x\in X
and
R\geq 0
,
we let
B(x,R)\coloneqq\{x^{\prime}\in X\mid d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})\leq R\}
be the ball with center
x
and radius
R\geq 0
.
Following
[
BKS20
]
*Definition 3.10, for any
R>0
, we let

\displaystyle C^{1}_{R}(S^{X})

\displaystyle\coloneqq C^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\cap\Bigl(\prod_{e\in E}C\bigl(S^{B(o(e),R)}\bigr)\Bigr).

Definition 2.28
.

We define the space of
uniform forms
by

\displaystyle C^{1}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{R>0}C^{1}_{R}(S^{X})\subset C^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu}).

Since the space of local functions
C(S^{B(o(e),R)})
is also a subset of
C(S^{X}_{*})
,
we may also view
C^{1}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})
as a subspace of
C^{1}(S^{X}_{*})
.
This space is defined independently of the choice of the probability measure
\mu
.

Lemma 2.29
.

If
f\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*})
, then we have
\partial f\in C^{1}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})
.

Proof.

Since
\nabla_{\!e}f^{*}_{\Lambda}\neq 0
only if
\{o(e),t(e)\}\cap\Lambda\neq\emptyset
,

\nabla_{\!e}f=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}\\
\{o(e),t(e)\}\cap\Lambda\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\nabla_{\!e}f^{*}_{\Lambda}.

Then, since
f
is uniform, the right hand side is
a finite sum and
\nabla_{\!e}f
is a local function. Moreover, if the diameter of
f
is
R
, then
\partial f\in C^{1}_{R+1}(S^{X})
, hence

\partial f
is a uniform form.
∎

Lemma 2.30
.

The isomorphism
\mathscr{R}
of (
8
) is compatible with the differential.
In other words, we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{\mathscr{R}}
\textstyle{C^{1}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{C^{1}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}).}

Proof.

Since
\partial
is linear, and since any
f\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{*})
by Proposition
2.16

may be expressed as a sum of
f^{*}_{\Lambda}

for
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
,
it is sufficient to prove our assertion for
f^{*}_{\Lambda}\in C(S^{\Lambda})
.
By definition of
\mathscr{R}
, we have

\mathscr{R}(f^{*}_{\Lambda})=\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\subset\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime\prime}\neq\emptyset}(f^{*}_{\Lambda})_{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}=f^{*}_{\Lambda}-E_{\mu}[f^{*}_{\Lambda}].

Our assertion now follows from the fact that the differential
\partial
of a constant is
zero
.
∎

Lemma
2.30
assures that for any uniform function
f\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})
or
C^{0}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
,
the differential
\partial f
is well-defined in
C^{1}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})
and does not depend on the representative of
f
.

Definition 2.31
.

We define the space of
closed uniform form
by

Z^{1}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})\coloneqq Z^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\cap C^{1}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X}).

2.4.
Norms on Functions and Forms

In this subsection, we define norms on the spaces of co-local functions and forms.
Recall that
\mu=\nu^{\otimes X}
is the product measure on
S^{X}
.
For any measurable function
f\in C(S^{X}_{\mu})
, we let

(15)

\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}\coloneqq E_{\mu}[f^{2}]=\int_{S^{X}}f^{2}d\mu.

Definition 2.32
.

The space of
L^{2}
-functions
L^{2}(\mu)
is given as the quotient

L^{2}(\mu)\coloneqq\{f\in C(S^{X}_{\mu})\mid\|f\|_{\mu}<\infty\}/\{f\in C(S^{X}_{\mu})\mid\|f\|_{\mu}=0\}

of the space of measurable functions on
S^{X}
bounded for the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mu}
divided
by the subspace of measurable functions with norm
zero
.

The space
L^{2}(\mu)
is a Hilbert space for the inner product

(16)

\langle f,g\rangle_{\mu}\coloneqq E_{\mu}[fg].

In particular,
L^{2}(\mu)
is complete for the topology given by the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mu}
.
Moreover, any function
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
is integrable for the measure
\mu
.
The conditional expectation
\pi^{\Lambda}
satisfies the following properties.

Lemma 2.33
.

For any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
,
the homomorphism
\pi^{\Lambda}
is an orthogonal projection of
L^{2}(\mu)
to
C(S^{\Lambda})

with respect to the inner product (
16
).

Proof.

The assertions follow directly from the definition of the conditional expectation.
Detailed proofs are given in
[
BS21
]
*Lemma 2.1.
∎

For any
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
, the system
(f^{\Lambda})_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}
for
f^{\Lambda}\coloneqq\pi^{\Lambda}f
defines
an element in
C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
. This shows that we have a homomorphism
L^{2}(\mu)\rightarrow C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.
The martingale convergence theorem for
L^{2}
-bounded martingales gives the following.

Theorem 2.34
.

Let

C_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\coloneqq\{(f^{\Lambda})_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}\in C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\mid\sup_{\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}}\|f^{\Lambda}\|_{\mu}<\infty\}.

Then
C_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
coincides with the image of
L^{2}(\mu)
in
C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.

Proof.

See
[
BS21
]
*Theorem 5.2 and
[
BS21
]
*Corollary 5.2 for a detailed proof.
∎

In what follows, we will identify
L^{2}(\mu)
with
C_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.
We next introduce norms on the space of forms
C^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.
We let
r=(r_{e})_{e\in E}
be a weight as in Definition
2.7
.
For any
e\in E
, we define a weighted
L^{2}
-norm for
f
in
C(S^{X}_{\mu})
as follows.

(17)

\|f\|_{r_{e}}^{2}\coloneqq E_{\mu}[r_{e}f^{2}]=\int_{S^{X}}r_{e}f^{2}d\mu.

If the weight
r=(r_{e})
is the
trivial weight
satisfying

r_{e}\equiv 1
for any
e\in E
, then the norm
\|\cdot\|_{r_{e}}

coincides with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mu}
of (
15
).

Definition 2.35
.

We define the space of
L^{2}
-functions
L^{2}(\mu)_{r_{e}}
to be the quotient

L^{2}(\mu)_{r_{e}}\coloneqq\{f\in C(S^{X}_{\mu})\mid\|f\|_{r_{e}}<\infty\}/\{f\in C(S^{X}_{\mu})\mid\|f\|_{r_{e}}=0\}.

The space
L^{2}(\mu)_{r_{e}}
is a Hilbert space for the inner product

(18)

\langle f,g\rangle_{r_{e}}\coloneqq E_{\mu}[r_{e}fg].

Since
r_{e}
is a positive local function, it is bounded from above and below by some positive constants. Hence the norms
\|\cdot\|_{r_{e}}

and
\|\cdot\|_{\mu}
are equivalent,
and we have
L^{2}(\mu)_{r_{e}}=L^{2}(\mu)
.
Again using the inclusion
C^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\hookrightarrow\prod_{e\in E}C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})

of (
10
),
we may define the space of
L^{2}
-forms as follows.

Definition 2.36
.

We define the
space of
L^{2}
-forms

C^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
by

(19)

C^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\coloneqq C^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\cap\Bigl(\prod_{e\in E}L^{2}(\mu)_{r_{e}}\Bigr),

which is independent from the choice of the weight
r=(r_{e})
.

The following constants will be used in the calculation of the bound of norms.

Definition 2.37
.

Let
r=(r_{e})
be a weight for the system
((X,E),(S,\phi))
.

(a)

For any
e\in E
, we define the
weight bound

to be the infimum
M_{r_{e}}
of constants
M\geq 1
satisfying

(20)

M^{-1}\leq r_{e}(\eta)\leq M

for any
\eta\in S^{X}
.
Such
M_{r_{e}}
exists since
r_{e}
is a positive
local function.

(b)

For any
e\in E
,
we define the
transition bound

to be the infimum
A_{r_{e}}
of constants
A\geq 1
satisfying

(21)

A^{-1}\leq\frac{(\pi^{\Lambda}r_{e})(\eta)\mu(\eta)}{(\pi^{\Lambda}r_{\bar{e}})(\eta^{e})\mu(\eta^{e})}\leq A

for any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
such that
e\in E_{\Lambda}
and
\eta\in S^{\Lambda}
.
Such
A_{r_{e}}
exists since
r_{e}
and
r_{\bar{e}}
are
local functions.

Remark 2.38
.

(a)

If the weight
r=(r_{e})
is the trivial weight
given by
r_{e}\equiv 1
for any
e\in E
, then
A_{r_{e}}=C_{\phi,\nu}
where
C_{\phi,\nu}
is the constant defined in Definition
2.9
.

(b)

For any weight
r=(r_{e})
and
e\in E
,
we see from the definition that
A_{r_{e}}\leq M_{r_{e}}M_{r_{\bar{e}}}C_{\phi,\nu}
.

Let
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
and
(\Lambda,E_{\Lambda})
be the corresponding finite locale.
For any
f\in C(S^{\Lambda})
and
e\in E_{\Lambda}
, recall that
\nabla_{\!e}f(\eta)=f(\eta^{e})-f(\eta)
for any
\eta\in S^{\Lambda}
.
We may prove the following.

Lemma 2.39
.

Let
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
and
e\in E_{\Lambda}
.
Then for any
f\in C(S^{\Lambda})
, we have

A_{r_{\bar{e}}}^{-1}\|\nabla_{\!\bar{e}}f\|_{r_{\bar{e}}}^{2}\leq\|\nabla_{\!e}f\|^{2}_{r_{e}}\leq A_{r_{e}}\|\nabla_{\!\bar{e}}f\|^{2}_{r_{\bar{e}}}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\!e}f\|^{2}_{r_{e}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\eta\in S^{\Lambda}}(f(\eta^{e})-f(\eta))^{2}(\pi^{\Lambda}r_{e})(\eta)\mu(\eta)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in S^{\Lambda}\\
\eta^{e}\neq\eta\end{subarray}}(f(\eta^{e})-f(\eta))^{2}(\pi^{\Lambda}r_{e})(\eta)\mu(\eta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in S^{\Lambda}\\
\eta^{e}\neq(\eta^{e})^{\bar{e}},\eta^{e}\neq\eta\end{subarray}}(f(\eta^{e})-f((\eta^{e})^{\bar{e}}))^{2}(\pi^{\Lambda}r_{e})(\eta)\mu(\eta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta^{\prime}\in S^{\Lambda}\\
\eta^{\prime}\neq(\eta^{\prime})^{\bar{e}}\end{subarray}}(f(\eta^{\prime})-f((\eta^{\prime})^{\bar{e}}))^{2}(\pi^{\Lambda}r_{e})((\eta^{\prime})^{\bar{e}})\mu((\eta^{\prime})^{\bar{e}})

\displaystyle\leq A_{r_{e}}\sum_{\eta^{\prime}\in S^{\Lambda}}(f(\eta^{\prime})-f((\eta^{\prime})^{\bar{e}}))^{2}(\pi^{\Lambda}r_{\bar{e}})(\eta^{\prime})\mu(\eta^{\prime})=A_{r_{e}}\|\nabla_{\bar{e}}f\|^{2}_{r_{\bar{e}}}.

Here, we use the fact that the map
\eta\to\eta^{e}
is the bijection from
\{\eta\in S^{\Lambda};\eta^{e}\neq\eta\}
to
\{\eta^{\prime}\in S^{\Lambda};\eta^{\prime}\neq(\eta^{\prime})^{\bar{e}}\}
. This gives the second inequality of the statement. The first inequality follows by replacing
e
by
\bar{e}
.
∎

We may prove the continuity of
\nabla_{\!e}
as follows.

Lemma 2.40
.

For any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
,
f\in C(S^{\Lambda})
and
e\in E_{\Lambda}
, we have

\|\nabla_{\!e}f\|^{2}_{r_{e}}\leq 4M_{r_{e}}C_{\phi,\nu}\|f\|^{2}_{\mu}.

In particular,
\nabla_{\!e}
gives a continuous map from
L^{2}(\mu)
to
L^{2}(\mu)_{r_{e}}
.

Proof.

By definition, we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\!e}f\|^{2}_{r_{e}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in S^{\Lambda}\\
\eta^{e}\neq\eta\end{subarray}}\bigl(f(\eta^{e})-f(\eta)\bigr)^{2}\pi^{\Lambda}r_{e}(\eta)\mu(\eta)\leq M_{r_{e}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in S^{\Lambda}\\
\eta^{e}\neq\eta\end{subarray}}\bigl(f(\eta^{e})-f(\eta)\bigr)^{2}\mu(\eta)

\displaystyle\leq 2M_{r_{e}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in S^{\Lambda}\\
\eta^{e}\neq\eta\end{subarray}}\bigl(f(\eta^{e})^{2}\mu(\eta)+f(\eta)^{2}\mu(\eta)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2M_{r_{e}}C_{\phi,\nu}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in S^{\Lambda}\\
\eta^{e}\neq\eta\end{subarray}}\bigl(f(\eta^{e})^{2}\mu(\eta^{e})+f(\eta)^{2}\mu(\eta)\bigr)

\displaystyle=2M_{r_{e}}C_{\phi,\nu}\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in S^{\Lambda}\\
\eta^{\bar{e}}\neq\eta\end{subarray}}f(\eta)^{2}\mu(\eta)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in S^{\Lambda}\\
\eta^{e}\neq\eta\end{subarray}}f(\eta)^{2}\mu(\eta)\Bigr)\leq 4M_{r_{e}}C_{\phi,\nu}\|f\|^{2}_{\mu},

where the first inequality follows from the definition of the weight bound
M_{r_{e}}

given in Definition
2.37
, the second inequality follows from Schwartz inequality, and the third inequality follows from the definition of
C_{\phi,\nu}
given in Definition
2.9
.
∎

Next, we introduce the notion of the
spectral gap
,
which plays an important role in the assumption for our main theorem.
Let
(\Lambda,E_{\Lambda})
be a finite locale, with interaction
(S,\phi)

and a product measure
\mu=\nu^{\otimes\Lambda}
.
For any
\Lambda\in\mathscr{I}
, we denote by
\|\cdot\|_{\Lambda}
the norm on

A^{0}(S^{\Lambda})\coloneqq C(S^{\Lambda})/{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\partial_{\Lambda}

induced from the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mu}
on
C(S^{\Lambda})
as
\|f\|_{\Lambda}=\inf_{g\in{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\partial_{\Lambda}}\|f-g\|_{\mu}
.
In general,
\|h\|_{\Lambda}\leq\|h\|_{\mu}
, and we have
\|h\|_{\Lambda}=\|h\|_{\mu}

if and only if
h\in({\mathrm{Ker}\,}\partial_{\Lambda})^{\perp}
, where
({\mathrm{Ker}\,}\partial_{\Lambda})^{\perp}

is the orthogonal complement of
{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\partial_{\Lambda}
for the inner product (
16
).

To define the spectral gap, we first give a simple lemma.

Lemma 2.41
.

For any finite locale
(\Lambda,E_{\Lambda})
, there exists a constant
C>0
which
depends on the locale
(\Lambda,E_{\Lambda})
and the weight
r=(r_{e})
such that for any
f\in A^{0}(S^{\Lambda})
,

\|f\|^{2}_{\Lambda}\leq C\sum_{e\in E_{\Lambda}}\|\nabla_{\!e}f\|^{2}_{r_{e}}.

Proof.

Since
({\mathrm{Ker}\,}\partial_{\Lambda})^{\perp}
is a finite dimensional space and the quadratic form
f\mapsto\sum_{e\in E_{\Lambda}}\|\nabla_{\!e}f\|^{2}_{r_{e}}
does not degenerate on
({\mathrm{Ker}\,}\partial_{\Lambda})^{\perp}
, we have

\inf_{f\in({\mathrm{Ker}\,}\partial_{\Lambda})^{\perp},\|f\|^{2}_{\mu}=1}\sum_{e\in E_{\Lambda}}\|\nabla_{\!e}f\|^{2}_{r_{e}}>0.

This implies the assertion.
∎

To state our assumption for the main theorem, we only need to introduce the notion of the spectral gap associated to the trivial weight
r_{e}\equiv 1
as follows.

Definition 2.42
.

For an interaction
(S,\phi)
, a finite locale
(\Lambda,E_{\Lambda})
and a probability measure
\nu
on
S
supported on
S
, we define the spectral gap
C_{SG,(\Lambda,E_{\Lambda})}
(which also depend on
(S,\phi)
and
\nu
) to be the maximum of
C>0
satisfying

\|f\|^{2}_{\Lambda}\leq C^{-1}\sum_{e\in E_{\Lambda}}\|\nabla_{\!e}f\|^{2}_{\mu}

for any
f\in A^{0}(S^{\Lambda})
where
\mu=\nu^{\otimes\Lambda}
.

Finally, we introduce a norm on the space of
L^{2}
-forms
C^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.

Definition 2.43
.

We define the norm
\|\cdot\|_{r,{\mathrm{sp}}}
to be the norm on
C^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
defined by

\|\omega\|_{r,{\mathrm{sp}}}\coloneqq\sup_{e\in E}\|\omega_{e}\|_{r_{e}},

and we let

C^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})_{r,{\mathrm{sp}}}

be the subspace of
C^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
given as

C^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})_{r,{\mathrm{sp}}}\coloneqq\{\omega\in C^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})\mid\|\omega\|_{r,{\mathrm{sp}}}<\infty\}.

Furthermore, we let
Z^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})_{r,{\mathrm{sp}}}\coloneqq C^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})_{r,{\mathrm{sp}}}\cap Z^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.

We remark that although
C^{1}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})\subset C^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
, we have in general

C^{1}_{\mathrm{unif}}(S^{X})\not\subset C^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})_{r,{\mathrm{sp}}}.

Certain homogeneity condition such as shift-invariance of the weight as well as the form is
necessary for an uniform form to be an element of
C^{1}_{L^{2}}(S^{X}_{\mu})_{r,{\mathrm{sp}}}
.
We will simply denote
\|\cdot\|_{r,{\mathrm{sp}}}
by
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{sp}}
, if the weight
r

is the trivial weight such that
r_{e}\equiv 1
for any
e\in E
.

3.
The Main Theorem

In this section, we introduce the norms on shift-invariant forms, and state our main theorem.

3.1.
Closed and Exact Shift-Invariant
L^{2}
-Forms

In this subsection, in order to formulate our main theorem, we introduce the space
of closed and exact shift-invariant
L^{2}
-forms on
S^{X}
.
We will also introduce the notion of a uniformly bounded spectral gap.

In this subsection, we assume that the locale
(X,E)
has an action of a group
G
.
An action of
G
on
(X,E)
gives for any
\tau\in G
a bijection
\tau\colon X\rightarrow X

inducing a bijection
\tau\colon E\rightarrow E
on the set of directed edges,
such that the identity element of
G
induces the identity map of
X
,
and the operation of elements of
G
maps to the composition of bijections.
In the rest of the paper, we always assume that the action of
G
is free, and that the set
X/G
of orbits
of
X
with respect to the action of
G
is a finite set.
In this case, the set
E/G
of orbits
of
E
with respect to the action of
G
is also a finite set.
The most typical example of such locale is given by the
Euclidean lattice

(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\mathbb{E}^{d})
,
with action of
G=\mathbb{Z}^{d}
given by translation. A locale which is a
topological crystal

in the sense of
[
Sun13
]
*§6.3, also referred to as a
crystal lattice
, satisfies this condition for some finitely generated free abelian group
G
.

The action of
G
on
(X,E)
induces an action of
G
on

S^{X}
given by mapping
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in X}\in\prod_{x\in X}S
to

\tau(\eta)\coloneqq(\eta_{\tau^{-1}(x)})_{x\in X}
for any
\tau\in G
.
For any subset
\Lambda\subset X
, the element
\tau\in G
induces
an
\mathbb{R}
-linear isomorphism

(22)

\tau\colon C\bigl(S^{\Lambda}\bigr)\xrightarrow{\cong}C\bigl(S^{\tau(\Lambda)}\bigr)

given by

\tau(f)(\eta)=f(\tau^{-1}(\eta))

for any
f\in C\bigl(S^{\Lambda}\bigr)
and
\eta\in S^{\tau(\Lambda)}
.
Furthermore, the action of
G
defines a map of graphs

\tau\colon(S^{\Lambda},\Phi_{\Lambda})\rightarrow(S^{\tau(\Lambda)},\Phi_{\tau(\Lambda)})
,
which induces an
\mathbb{R}
-linear isomorphism

\tau\colon C^{1}(S^{\Lambda})\xrightarrow{\cong}C^{1}(S^{\tau(\Lambda)}),

given by
\tau(\omega)(\varphi)\coloneqq\omega(\tau^{-1}(\varphi))
for any
\omega\in C^{1}(S^{\Lambda})
and

\varphi\in\Phi_{\Lambda}
.
Consider the image of
\omega
in
\prod_{e\in E_{\Lambda}}C(S^{\Lambda})
through the embedding
(
9
).
For any
\tau\in G
, we have

\tau(\omega)_{e}(\eta)=\tau(\omega)(\varphi)=\omega(\tau^{-1}(\varphi))=\omega_{\tau^{-1}(e)}(\tau^{-1}(\eta))=\tau(\omega_{\tau^{-1}(e)})(\eta),

where
\varphi=(\eta,\eta^{e})\in\Phi_{\Lambda}
.
This shows that we have
\tau(\omega)_{e}=\tau(\omega_{\tau^{-1}(e)})
for any
e\in E_{\Lambda}
.

Since
\mu=\nu^{\otimes X}
on
S^{X}
,
we have

\mu(A)=\mu(\tau(A))
for any
A\in\mathcal{F}_{X}
and
\tau\in G
.
In other words,
\mu
is invariant with respect to the action of
G
.
The action of
G
is compatible with the projection
\pi^{\Lambda}
,
hence we have an action of
G
on
C_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
and
C^{1}_{\mathrm{col}}(S^{X}_{\mu})
.
Since
\mu
is invariant with respect to the action of
G
, we have
